Nonlinear Landau damping for the Vlasov-Poisson system in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} : the Poisson equilibrium Thanks: A.I. was supported in part by NSF grant DMS-2007008; B.P. was partially supported by a Simons Fellowship, the CY-IAS fellowship program, and NSF grant DMS-1700282; X.W. was supported in part by NSFC-12141102, and MOST-2020YFA0713003. K.W. gratefully acknowledges support of the SNSF through grant PCEFP2_203059. This material is based upon work supported by the National Science Foundation under Grant No. DMS-1929284 while A.I., B.P. and K.W. were in residence at the Institute for Computational and Experimental Research in Mathematics in Providence, RI, during the program “Hamiltonian Methods in Dispersive and Wave Evolution Equations”.
Alexandru D. Ionescu, Benoit Pausader, Xuecheng Wang, Klaus Widmayer

Abstract
. We prove asymptotic stability of the Poisson homogeneous equilibrium among solutions of the Vlasov-Poisson system in the Euclidean space ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} . More precisely, we show that small, smooth, and localized perturbations of the Poisson equilibrium lead to global solutions of the Vlasov-Poisson system, which scatter to linear solutions at a polynomial rate as t → ∞ t\to\infty . The Euclidean problem we consider here differs significantly from the classical work on Landau damping in the periodic setting, in several ways. Most importantly, the linearized problem cannot satisfy a “Penrose condition”. As a result, our system contains resonances (small divisors) and the electric field is a superposition of an electrostatic component and a larger oscillatory component, both with polynomially decaying rates.



1.
Introduction

It is believed that the vast majority of ordinary matter in the visible universe takes the form of a plasma, i.e. of an ionised gas, ranging from sparse intergalactic plasma to the interior of stars and neon
signs. Both from a theoretical and practical (e.g. nuclear fusion) point of view, the understanding of stability versus instability in plasmas is a formidable yet crucial challenge. We refer to e.g.
[
10
,
7
]
for physics references in book form.

In this article we consider a
hot, unconfined, electrostatic
plasma in three dimensions of electrons on a uniform, static, background of ions. Here collisions are neglected and the associated distribution of electrons is modeled by a measure
M(x,v,t)dxdv
on the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}_{v}
, where
M
satisfies the
Vlasov-Poisson
equation.

m_{e}(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})M+q{\bf E}\cdot\nabla_{v}M=0,\qquad\hbox{div}_{x}{\bf E}(x,t)=4\pi\epsilon_{0}\left\{n_{0}e+q\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}Mdv\right\}.

(1.1)

In (
1.1
),
{\bf E}
denotes the self-generated electrostatic field,
q=-e<0
the charge of an electron,
m_{e}
its mass,
\epsilon_{0}
the vacuum resistivity and
n_{0}e>0
the uniform background charge density of the ions.

A particularly simple yet relevant class of solutions to (
1.1
) are stationary,
spatially homogeneous
functions
M_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to[0,\infty)
satisfying
q\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}M_{0}\,dv=n_{0}
. To understand the role of such equilibria in the overall dynamics of the Vlasov-Poisson equations, an important step is the investigation of their stability. Writing

M(x,v,t)=n_{0}e^{-1}\left(M_{0}(v)+F(x,v,t)\right)

(1.2)

the equation for the perturbation
F
becomes

\begin{split}\left(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)F+\omega^{2}_{e}\nabla_{x}\psi\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}&+\omega_{e}^{2}\nabla_{x}\psi\cdot\nabla_{v}F=0,\qquad\Delta_{x}\psi=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}Fdv,\end{split}

(1.3)

with electrostatic potential
\psi
and
electron plasma frequency

\omega_{e}
given by
\omega_{e}^{2}:=\frac{4\pi\epsilon_{0}n_{0}e^{2}}{m_{e}}
. Let

f(x,v,t):=F\big(\frac{x}{\omega_{e}},v,\frac{t}{\omega_{e}}\big),\qquad\phi(x,t):=\omega_{e}^{2}\cdot\psi\big(\frac{x}{\omega_{e}},\frac{t}{\omega_{e}}\big),

so we obtain the nondimensionalized Vlasov-Poisson system

\begin{split}\left(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)f+\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}f&=-\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0},\\
\Delta_{x}\phi(x,t)&=\rho(x,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(x,v,t)\,dv,\end{split}

(1.4)

which will be the focus of the rest of this article.

1.1.
Main result

In this paper we investigate the asymptotic stability of a particular homogeneous equilibrium, namely the “Poisson equilibrium”

M_{0}(v):=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}(1+|v|^{2})^{2}},\qquad\widehat{M_{0}}(\xi)=e^{-|\xi|}.

(1.5)

Our main result asserts that smooth perturbations of
M_{0}
lead to global solutions which scatter to linear solutions, and exhibit two different dynamics in their associated electric fields:

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists
\overline{\varepsilon}>0
such that if the initial particle distribution
f_{0}
satisfies

\sum_{\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq 1}\left\|\langle v\rangle^{4.5}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f_{0}(x,v)\right\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}L^{\infty}_{v}}+\left\|\langle v\rangle^{4.5}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f_{0}(x,v)\right\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{v}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\leq\overline{\varepsilon},

(1.6)

then the Vlasov-Poisson system (
1.4
) with
M_{0}
defined as in (
1.5
) has a global unique solution
f\in C^{1}_{x,v,t}(\mathbb{R}^{3+3}\times\mathbb{R}_{+})
that scatters linearly, i.e. there exists
f_{\infty}\in L^{\infty}_{x,v}
such that

\begin{split}\|f(x,v,t)-f_{\infty}(x-tv,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x,v}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-1/2}.\end{split}

(1.7)

Moreover, the electric field
E(x,t)=\nabla_{x}\phi(x,t)
decomposes into a “static” and an “oscillatory” component with different decay rates

\begin{split}&E(t)=E^{stat}(t)+\Re(e^{-it}E^{osc}(t)),\\
&\langle t\rangle\left\|E^{stat}(t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|E^{osc}(t)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta},\end{split}

(1.8)

where
\delta=10^{-4}
is a small parameter.

Remark 1.2
.

(1) Theorem
1.1
appears to be the first nonlinear asymptotic stability result for the Vlasov-Poisson system in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in a neighborhood of a smooth non-trivial equilibrium (see
[
40
]
for the case of a repulsive point charge). We work with the Poisson homogeneous equilibrium
M_{0}
mostly for the sake of simplicity, as it leads to explicit formulas such as (
1.15
)–(
1.16
). However, we expect that the linear scattering conclusion of the theorem and the underlying analysis extend to more general smooth homogeneous equilibria, at least as long as the resonances of the system (the set where the kernel
1+K(\xi,\lambda)
defined as in (
1.11
) vanishes) are not too severe. See sections
1.2
and
1.3
for further discussion.

(2) The statement of the theorem, in particular the crucial decay estimates (
1.8
), depend on the fact that we work in dimension
d=3
. The decay is stronger in higher dimensions
d\geq 4
(so the proof becomes easier), and weaker and insufficient in dimension
d=2
. This is due mainly to the dimension-dependent dispersive estimates Lemma
3.4
, and is in sharp contrast with the periodic case
x\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
. See section
1.2
for further discussion.

1.2.
Prior works

The literature of broadly related results is too vast to be surveyed here, so we will focus on a selection of more directly related results. In particular, we restrict our attention to the setting of three spatial dimensions.

1.2.1.
Perturbations of vacuum

The simplest equilibrium of (
1.1
) is the
vacuum

n_{0}\equiv 0
and
M_{0}\equiv 0
. The resulting equations

\left(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\right)f+\lambda\nabla_{x}\phi\cdot\nabla_{v}f=0,\qquad\Delta_{x}\phi(x,t)=\int f(x,v,t)dv,\qquad\lambda\in\{-1,1\},

(1.9)

are also relevant in astrophysics, where the self-interactions through a gravitational potential are attractive (
\lambda=-1
) rather than repulsive (
\lambda=+1
). In the former case, (
1.9
) possesses a large variety of localized equilibria, see e.g.
[
21
,
33
,
43
,
38
]
.

For sufficiently localized initial data, the system (
1.9
) has been extensively studied (see e.g.
[
15
,
43
]
for book references). Solutions are global in time under reasonably general regularity assumptions
[
1
,
36
,
44
,
42
]
. For small perturbations, the electric field and charge density decay over time
[
1
]
, but the precise dynamics were only recently clarified (after a series of preliminary works
[
26
,
41
,
24
,
45
,
9
,
46
]
): the
long-range effects
of the electrostatic field lead to asymptotic dynamics that feature a logarithmic correction of linear scattering, a phenomenon known as
modified scattering

[
31
,
11
]
.

1.2.2.
Nonlinear Landau damping

As is well-known, the free transport equation
\partial_{t}f+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f=0
exhibits
phase mixing
, which can manifest itself as time decay in the spatial density
\rho(t,x)
. It was an observation of Landau
[
32
]
(see also
[
34
, Chapter 3, Section 30]
) that an interesting mechanism of decay exists also in the linearized Vlasov-Poisson equations near homogeneous equilibria satisfying certain conditions (nowadays called “the Penrose criterion”).

\bullet
The periodic setting
x\in\mathbb{T}^{3}
, is a natural model for a
confined
plasma and has been studied extensively. After some preliminary works
[
8
,
25
]
,
nonlinear Landau damping
was proved in the pioneering work
[
39
]
(see also
[
3
,
16
]
for refinements and simplifications). More precisely, for suitable homogeneous equilibria
M_{0}
that satisfy the
Penrose criterion

\inf_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{3}\setminus\{0\},\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\,\Im(\lambda)<0}\left\lvert 1+\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\lambda s}s\widehat{M_{0}}(ks)ds\right\rvert>0,

(1.10)

it was shown that small, highly regular (analytic or Gevrey) perturbations lead to a global solution which
scatters linearly
, with an electric field that decays exponentially.

The confined nature of the system induces strong
nonlinear echoes
which, for smooth enough perturbations, are compensated by the fast decay of the linearized equation. Rougher perturbations (e.g. Sobolev) can lead to other stationary solutions
[
6
,
35
]
or still damp
[
17
]
. We note that related mechanisms are at play in
2d

fluid mixing
, in particular near monotone shear flows
[
2
,
28
,
29
,
37
]
or point vortices
[
30
]
.

\bullet
In the
unconfined
setting
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, Theorem
1.1
is the first nonlinear asymptotic stability result of non-trivial, smooth homogeneous equilibria in 3D. Previous results are mainly concerned with the linearized system, see
[
13
,
14
]
, and the screened case, see
[
4
,
22
]
, in which the low frequency part is screened out.

The key difficulty for the unconfined case is caused by the fact that the Penrose criterion cannot hold. More precisely, for any normalized homogeneous equilibrium
M_{0}
we have

\begin{split}&1+K(0,\lambda)=1-\lambda^{-2}\,\,\,\text{ if }\,\,\,\Im(\lambda)<0\,\,\,\text{thus}\,\,\,\lim_{\xi\to 0,\,\lambda\to\pm 1,\,\Im(\lambda)<0}\left\lvert 1+K(\xi,\lambda)\right\rvert=0,\\
&\text{where}\qquad K(\xi,\lambda):=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-i\lambda s}s\widehat{M_{0}}(\xi s)\,ds.\end{split}

(1.11)

In particular, the key lower bound in the Penrose criterion (
1.10
) cannot hold. This critical difficulty has already been observed in
[
13
,
14
,
5
,
23
]
in the study of the linearized system.

In the nonlinear setting the failure of the Penrose condition leads to the presence of small denominators and resonances in the system. As a consequence we have much slower decay and convergence (depending on the dimension of the ambient space), and the global dynamics is completely different compared to the periodic case. In fact our analysis reveals two different types of dynamics for the electric field: an oscillatory part, which decays at almost the critical rate
\langle t\rangle^{-2}
and oscillates like
e^{-it}
over time, and a static part, which decays faster than the critical rate
\langle t\rangle^{-2}
. See section
1.3
below for more details.

We note that a suitable analogue of the Penrose condition (
1.10
) still holds in the screened case investigated in
[
4
,
22
]
. As a consequence there are no resonances, and one can prove sufficiently rapid decay of the electric field (of order
\langle t\rangle^{-4+}
) for smooth perturbations. For comparison, the decay of the electric field in the case of vacuum perturbations is of the order of
\langle t\rangle^{-2}
.

1.2.3.
Comparison with Euler-Poisson models

Finally, we note the analogy with the related case of
warm plasmas
, when one can use fluid models instead of kinetic ones: the Euler-Poisson equation for electron is classically asymptotically stable (for irrotational data)
[
20
]
, while the stability of the ion equation was more recently obtained
[
19
]
. In this case, the two-fluid models involving both electrons and ions lead to new and interesting phenomena
[
12
,
27
,
18
]
.

1.3.
Main ideas

As in
[
22
]
we use a Lagrangian approach. The left-hand side of (
1.4
) is a transport equation for
f
, the (backwards) characteristics of which are the curves

\displaystyle\partial_{s}X(x,v,s,t)

\displaystyle=V(x,v,s,t),\qquad

\displaystyle X(x,v,t,t)

\displaystyle=x,

(1.12)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}V(x,v,s,t)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{x}\phi(X(x,v,s,t),s),\qquad

\displaystyle V(x,v,t,t)

\displaystyle=v.

Notice that we can integrate (
1.4
) to obtain an exact formula for
f(x,v,t)
, namely

\begin{split}f(x,v,t)&=f_{0}(X(x,v,0,t),V(x,v,0,t))-\int_{0}^{t}\nabla_{x}\phi(X(x,v,s,t),s)\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}(V(x,v,s,t))ds.\end{split}

(1.13)

It thus suffices to study the characteristics, which are given through the electric field
\nabla_{x}\phi
, which in turn arises from the density
\rho
. Through integration in
v
in (
1.13
), together with (
1.12
) we recast (
1.4
) as a closed system for the density
\rho
satisfying

\rho(x,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(t-s)\rho(x-(t-s)v,s)M_{0}(v)\,dvds=\mathcal{N}(x,t),

(1.14)

where
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{1}+\mathcal{N}_{2}
is a sum of initial data contribution
\mathcal{N}_{1}
and a nonlinear expression
\mathcal{N}_{2}
in
\rho
(see (
2.5
)-(
2.6
) below for precise formulas). This formulation is amenable to a bootstrap approach that we detail further.

1.3.1.
Linear analysis and the Penrose condition

The (linear) left-hand side of (
1.13
) is a Volterra equation, which can be integrated exactly. Using the Laplace-Fourier transform and the explicit formula (
1.5
), after a few algebraic manipulations it follows from (
1.14
) that

\begin{split}&\left(1+K(\xi,\lambda)\right)\widetilde{\rho}(\xi,\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,\lambda),\\
&1+K(\xi,\lambda)=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}r\widehat{M_{0}}(r\xi)e^{-i\lambda r}\,dr=\frac{(|\xi|+i(\lambda-1))(|\xi|+i(\lambda+1))}{(\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert+i\lambda)^{2}}.\end{split}

(1.15)

Notice that there is no uniform lower bound for
|1+K|
as
\xi\to 0
and
\lambda\to\pm 1
, i.e. a Penrose condition similar to (
1.10
) cannot be satisfied. However, we can still obtain an explicit solution of the linear problem,

\widehat{\rho}(\xi,t)=\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,t)-\int_{0}^{t}\widehat{\mathcal{N}}(\xi,\tau)e^{-(t-\tau)|\xi|}\sin(t-\tau)\,d\tau.

(1.16)

This gives a first “solution formula” (in the same spirit as a Duhamel formula) for
\rho
in terms of the initial data and a nonlinear expression of itself.

We write
\sin(t-\tau)=\sin(t)\cos(\tau)-\cos(t)\sin(\tau)
and do integration by parts in
\tau
in (
1.16
) (similar to a normal form analysis) when the oscillation effect is strong, i.e. in the case
|v||\xi|\ll 1
. As a result, we derive
a second formula for the localized density, see (
2.27
)-(
2.29
).

The identity (
1.16
) naturally leads to a decomposition of the density

\rho=\Re(e^{-it}\rho^{osc})+\rho^{stat}

(1.17)

into an oscillatory mode
\Re(e^{-it}\rho^{osc})
and a static component
\rho^{stat}
. The corresponding decomposition at the level of the electric field follows by setting
E^{\ast}=\nabla\Delta^{-1}\rho^{\ast}
,
\ast\in\{osc,stat\}
.

1.3.2.
The bootstrap argument and nonlinear analysis

Our main bootstrap argument is at the level of the density function
\rho
. The main idea is to use the decomposition (
1.17
) and bound the two components
\rho^{osc}
and
\rho^{stat}
in two different Banach spaces: a stronger space for the static component
\rho^{stat}
and a weaker space for the oscillatory component
\rho^{osc}
. The choice of these two spaces is very important, see (
2.38
)–(
2.39
) for the precise definitions.
1
1

1

The choice of these bootstrap spaces is in fact the most important choice in the paper, in the spirit of the “
Z
-norm method” used by the authors in earlier work
[
27
,
18
,
31
]
.
The point is that even though the oscillatory component
\rho^{osc}
satisfies weaker bounds, the presence of the factor
e^{-it}
allows integration by parts in time arguments (normal forms), which lead to improved decay and convergence.

To prove the main bootstrap proposition
2.4
we start from the identity (
1.16
). The main nonlinear contribution comes from the
reaction term

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{2}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big\{E(x-(t-s)v,s)\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}(v)\\
&-E(X(x,v,s,t),s)\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}(V(x,v,s,t))\big\}\,dvds,\end{split}

(1.18)

where the characteristics
(X,V)(x,v,s,t)
are defined as in (
1.12
).

The bootstrap assumptions on
\rho
and the definitions (
1.12
) can be used to derive bounds on the deviations
V(x,v,s,t)-v
and
X(x,v,s,t)-[x-(t-s)v]
of the linear characteristics. To estimate the reaction term we further localize in
|v|\approx 2^{j}
and
x
-frequency
|\xi|\approx 2^{k}
. We then examine the resulting nonlinear interactions, and analyze them according to the size of the parameters
j
and
k
. In some cases it is beneficial to integrate by parts in time (normal forms) or in
v
(improved dispersive estimates), particularly when the oscillatory components
E^{osc}
are involved. The analysis of the reaction term (
1.18
) is the most elaborate part of our proof and covers sections 5, 6, and 7.

Integration by parts in time produces quadratic “main terms” and a large number of “cubic remainders”. Fortunately, in many cases the cubic remainders are of lower order and we can deal with them in systematic fashion using Lemma
5.1
.

1.3.3.
General equilibria

It is natural to ask if the main conclusions of Theorem
1.1
hold for more general homogeneous equilibria
M_{0}(v)
. Our choice of the Poisson equilibrium simplifies the analysis, since it leads to explicit formulas such as (
1.15
)–(
1.16
), but we expect that the framework we construct here extends to more general situations.

At the technical level, in the case of more general homogeneous equilibria there is an additional difficulty identified in
[
5
,
23
]
: even at the linearized level the oscillatory component of the electric field contains an entire family of frequency dependent oscillations instead of just the two discrete modes we have here. This requires a more careful decomposition of the density function and more precise
Z
-norm analysis. We hope to return to these issues soon.

1.4.
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows: in section 2 we derive our main formulas for the density function, identify precisely its decomposition (Corollary
2.3
), and state our main bootstrap proposition (Proposition
2.4
). In section 3 we use the bootstrap assumptions to prove bounds on the modified characteristics (Lemmas
3.7
and
3.8
), general dispersive estimates (Lemma
3.4
and
3.5
), and bounds on operators defined by Fourier multipliers (Lemma
3.3
). In section 4 we prove improved bootstrap estimates on the contributions of the initial data. In sections 5, 6, and 7 we prove improved bootstrap estimates on the contributions of the reaction term. Finally, in section 8 we complete the proof of the main Theorem
1.1
.

2.
Dynamics of the density and bootstrap setup

Recall from (
1.4
) that the perturbation
f
we study satisfies the equation

\begin{split}&(\partial_{t}+v\cdot\nabla_{x})f+E\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}+E\cdot\nabla_{v}f=0,\\
&\rho(x,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f(x,v,t)\,dv,\qquad E:=\nabla_{x}\Delta_{x}^{-1}\rho,\end{split}

(2.1)

where

M_{0}(v):=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}(1+|v|^{2})^{2}},\qquad\widehat{M_{0}}(\xi)=e^{-|\xi|}.

(2.2)

To recast this as a problem for the density
\rho
we introduce the “backwards characteristics” of (
2.1
): these are the functions
X,V:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{I}^{2}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
obtained by solving the ODE system

\displaystyle\partial_{s}X(x,v,s,t)=V(x,v,s,t),\qquad

\displaystyle X(x,v,t,t)

\displaystyle=x,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}V(x,v,s,t)=E(X(x,v,s,t),s),\qquad

\displaystyle V(x,v,t,t)

\displaystyle=v,

where
\mathcal{I}^{2}_{T}:=\{(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}:\,s\leq t\}
. The main equation (
2.1
) gives

\begin{split}\frac{d}{ds}f(X(x,v,s,t),V(x,v,s,t),s)&=[(\partial_{s}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}+E\cdot\nabla_{v})f](X(x,v,s,t),V(x,v,s,t),s)\\
&=-E(X(x,v,s,t),s)\cdot\nabla_{v}M_{0}(V(x,v,s,t)).\end{split}

Integrating over
s\in[0,t]
and letting
M^{\prime}_{0}:=\nabla_{v}M_{0}
, we have

f(x,v,t)=f_{0}(X(x,v,0,t),V(x,v,0,t))-\int_{0}^{t}E(X(x,v,s,t),s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{0}(V(x,v,s,t))\,ds,

(2.4)

for any
(x,v,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]
. Adding
\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}E(x-(t-s)v,s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{0}(v)
on both sides of (
2.4
), a further integration in
v
shows that

\rho(x,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}(t-s)\rho(x-(t-s)v,s)M_{0}(v)\,dvds=\mathcal{N}(x,t),

(2.5)

for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]
, where

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}(x,t)&:=\mathcal{N}_{1}(x,t)+\mathcal{N}_{2}(x,t),\\
\mathcal{N}_{1}(x,t)&:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}f_{0}(X(x,v,0,t),V(x,v,0,t))\,dv,\\
\mathcal{N}_{2}(x,t)&:=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big\{E(x-(t-s)v,s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{0}(v)-E(X(x,v,s,t),s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{0}(V(x,v,s,t))\big\}\,dvds.\end{split}

(2.6)

Since
E=\nabla_{x}\Delta_{x}^{-1}\rho
can be recovered directly from the density
\rho
, together with the characteristic equations (
2.3
), these equations yield a closed system.

2.1.
Solving the density equation

In view of (
2.5
), the Fourier transform
\widehat{\rho}
of the density satisfies a forced Volterra equation

\begin{split}\widehat{\rho}(\xi,t)+\int_{0}^{t}(t-s)\widehat{M}_{0}((t-s)\xi)\widehat{\rho}(\xi,s)ds=\widehat{H}(\xi,t),\qquad t\geq 0,\end{split}

(2.7)

where
H
is a forcing term. Such equations can be solved as follows:

Lemma 2.1
.

Assume
\rho
and
H
satisfy (
2.7
) and define the convolution kernel
G
by

\begin{split}G(\xi,\tau)&:=\delta_{0}(\tau)-\sin(\tau)e^{-\tau|\xi|}\mathbf{1}_{+}(\tau),\end{split}

(2.8)

where
\mathbf{1}_{+}
denotes the characteristic function of the interval
[0,\infty)
. Then

\begin{split}\widehat{\rho}(\xi,t)&=\int_{0}^{t}G(\xi,s)\widehat{H}(\xi,t-s)\,ds.\end{split}

(2.9)

Proof.

Recall the formulas (
2.2
). For any
\xi\neq 0
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
\Im\lambda\leq 0
we have

K(\xi,\lambda):=\int_{0}^{\infty}r\widehat{M_{0}}(r\xi)e^{-i\lambda r}\,dr=\int_{0}^{\infty}re^{-r(|\xi|+i\lambda)}\,dr=\frac{1}{(|\xi|+i\lambda)^{2}}.

(2.10)

Then (
2.7
) shows that

\mathcal{F}_{t}(\widehat{\rho}(\xi,t))(\lambda)+K(\xi,\lambda)\mathcal{F}_{t}(\widehat{\rho}(\xi,t))(\lambda)=\mathcal{F}_{t}(\widehat{H}(\xi,t))(\lambda).

(2.11)

The claim follows by computing that

\frac{1}{1+K(\xi,\lambda)}=1-\frac{1}{(|\xi|+i\lambda)^{2}+1}=1-\frac{1}{2i}\Big[\frac{1}{|\xi|+i(\lambda-1)}-\frac{1}{|\xi|+i(\lambda+1)}\Big],

(2.12)

so

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}^{-1}\Big(\frac{1}{1+K(\xi,\lambda)}\Big)(\tau)=\delta_{0}(\tau)-e^{-\tau|\xi|}\sin\tau\mathbf{1}_{+}(\tau)=:G(\xi,\tau),

(2.13)

as desired.
∎

Using this we can solve the equations for forcing terms as they appear in (
2.5
)-(
2.6
). This introduces the new, real operator

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(\nabla,v):=|\nabla_{x}|-v\cdot\nabla_{x},\end{split}

(2.14)

and reveals a discretely oscillatory dynamic:

Lemma 2.2
.

For a given
h:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
let

H(x,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(x-tv,v,t)dv.

(2.15)

Then we have two alternative expressions
\mathcal{S}^{I}[h]
and
\mathcal{S}^{II}[h]
for the solution of the associated equation (
2.7
), with

\mathcal{S}^{\ast}[h](x,t)=R^{\ast}(x,t)+\Re\{e^{-it}T^{\ast}(x,t)\},\qquad\ast\in\{I,II\},

(2.16)

where

\displaystyle R^{I}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(x-tv,v,t)dv,

(2.17)

\displaystyle T^{I}(x,t)

\displaystyle=-i\int_{0}^{t}e^{is}e^{-(t-s)|\nabla|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}h(x-sv,v,s)dvds,

and

\displaystyle R^{II}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}h(x-tv,v,t)dv,

(2.18)

\displaystyle T^{II}(x,t)

\displaystyle=e^{-t|\nabla|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{1-i\mathcal{D}}h(x,v,0)dv

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{e^{is}}{1-i\mathcal{D}}e^{-(t-s)|\nabla|}(\partial_{s}h)(x-sv,v,s)dvds.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

The expressions for
\mathcal{S}^{I}[h]
follow directly from the solution formula (
2.9
). For
\mathcal{S}^{II}[h]
, we have

\mathcal{F}\{h(\cdot-vt,v,t)\}=e^{-itv\cdot\xi}\widehat{h}(\xi,v,t),

(2.19)

and we observe that

\begin{split}G(\xi,\lambda)e^{i\lambda v\cdot\xi}\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}(\lambda)&=\frac{\mathcal{D}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}\delta_{0}(\lambda)+\frac{\cos(t)+\mathcal{D}\sin(t)}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}e^{-t\mathcal{D}}\delta_{0}(\lambda-t)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}\frac{d}{d\lambda}\left\{(\cos(\lambda)+\mathcal{D}\sin(\lambda))e^{-\lambda\mathcal{D}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,t]}(\lambda)\right\},\\
\end{split}

(2.20)

which gives the claim.
∎

2.2.
The main decomposition

Applying Lemma
2.2
to our equations (
2.5
)-(
2.6
), the density
\rho
naturally decomposes into “static” and “oscillatory” components. We will control these parts using a bootstrap argument and different norms.

To implement this, we further decompose the nonlinear terms of (
2.6
) in frequency, velocity space, and time. For this we fix an even smooth function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
supported in
[-8/5,8/5]
and equal to
1
in
[-5/4,5/4]
,
and define

\varphi_{k}(x):=\varphi(|x|/2^{k})-\varphi(|x|/2^{k-1}),\quad\varphi_{\leq k}(x):=\varphi(|x|/2^{k}),\quad\varphi_{>k}(x):=1-\varphi(x/2^{k}),

(2.21)

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 1
. Let
P_{k}
,
P_{\leq k}
, and
P_{>k}
denote the operators on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
defined by the Fourier multipliers
\varphi_{k}
,
\varphi_{\leq k}
, and
\varphi_{>k}
respectively.

Let
\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}=\varphi_{\leq 0}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}=\varphi_{j}
if
j\geq 1
. For any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
let

\varphi_{I}(x):=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\cap I}\varphi_{k}(x),\qquad\widetilde{\varphi}_{I}(x):=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\cap I}\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(x).

(2.22)

We define the functions
L_{1},L_{1,j}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
and
L_{2,j}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{I}_{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\begin{split}L_{1,j}(x,v,t)&:=\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)L_{1}(x,v,t),\quad L_{1}(x,v,t):=f_{0}(X(x+tv,v,0,t),V(x+tv,v,0,t)),\\
L_{2,j}(x,v,s,t)&:=E(x,s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{j}(v)-E(X(x+(t-s)v,v,s,t),s)\cdot M^{\prime}_{j}(V(x+(t-s)v,v,s,t)),\end{split}

(2.23)

where
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
M^{\prime}_{j}(v):=\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\nabla_{v}M_{0}(v)
. Then for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we define the functions
L_{1,j,k}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
and
L_{2,j,k}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{I}_{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

L_{1,j,k}(x,v,t):=P_{k}L_{1,j}(x,v,t),\qquad L_{2,j,k}(x,v,s,t):=P_{k}L_{2,j}(x,v,s,t),

(2.24)

where the projections
P_{k}
act in the
x
variable. It follows from (
2.6
) that

\begin{split}&\mathcal{N}_{1}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{N}_{1,j,k},\qquad\mathcal{N}_{1,j,k}(x,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}L_{1,j,k}(x-tv,v,t)\,dv,\\
&\mathcal{N}_{2}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{N}_{2,j,k},\qquad\mathcal{N}_{2,j,k}(x,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}L_{2,j,k}(x-(t-s)v,v,s,t)\,dvds.\end{split}

(2.25)

Contributions from initial data

For the contributions from
\mathcal{N}_{1}
, using the expressions
I
of Lemma
2.2
we thus we have the first decomposition

\begin{split}&\rho_{1,j,k}(x,t)=R^{I}_{1,j,k}(x,t)+\Re\big\{e^{-it}T^{I}_{1,j,k}(x,t)\big\},\\
&\mathcal{F}(R^{I}_{1,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,t)e^{-itv\cdot\xi}\,dv,\\
&\mathcal{F}(T^{I}_{1,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}(-i)e^{is}\cdot\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,dvds.\end{split}

(2.26)

Alternatively, formulation
II
of Lemma
2.2
yields a decomposition as

\rho_{1,j,k}(x,t)=R^{II}_{1,j,k}(x,t)+\Re\big\{e^{-it}T^{II}_{1,j,k}(x,t)\big\},

(2.27)

where

\mathcal{F}(R^{II}_{1,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,t)e^{-itv\cdot\xi}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}\,dv,

(2.28)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(T_{1,j,k}^{II})(\xi,t)&:=e^{-t|\xi|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{1-i\mathcal{D}}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,0)\,dv\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\frac{e^{is}}{1-i\mathcal{D}}(\partial_{s}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}})(\xi,v,s)\,dvds\end{split}

(2.29)

and we have slightly abused notation to denote by
\mathcal{D}
also its Fourier symbol

\mathcal{D}(\xi,v):=|\xi|-iv\cdot\xi.

(2.30)

Contributions from nonlinearity: “reaction terms”

Similarly, contributions from
\mathcal{N}_{2}
can be decomposed with formulation
I
of Lemma
2.2
to obtain

\begin{split}&\rho_{2,j,k}(x,t)=R^{I}_{2,j,k}(x,t)+\Re\big\{e^{-it}T_{2,j,k}^{I}(x,t)\big\},\\
&\mathcal{F}(R^{I}_{2,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{L_{2,j,k}}(\xi,v,\tau,t)e^{-i(t-\tau)v\cdot\xi}\,dvd\tau,\\
&\mathcal{F}(T^{I}_{2,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathbf{1}_{+}(s-\tau)e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}(-i)e^{is}\widehat{L_{2,j,k}}(\xi,v,\tau,s)e^{-i(s-\tau)v\cdot\xi}\,dvd\tau ds.\end{split}

(2.31)

Alternatively, formulation
II
gives the expressions

\rho_{2,j,k}(x,t)=R^{II}_{2,j,k}(x,t)+\Re\big\{e^{-it}T^{II}_{2,j,k}(x,t)\big\},

(2.32)

where

\mathcal{F}(R^{II}_{2,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\widehat{L_{2,j,k}}(\xi,v,\tau,t)e^{-i(t-\tau)v\cdot\xi}\frac{\mathcal{D}^{2}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}\,dvd\tau,

(2.33)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(T^{II}_{2,j,k})(\xi,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}&\mathbf{1}_{+}(s-\tau)e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-i(s-\tau)v\cdot\xi}\\
&\times\frac{e^{is}}{1-i\mathcal{D}}(\partial_{s}\widehat{L_{2,j,k}})(\xi,v,\tau,s)\,dvd\tau ds.\end{split}

(2.34)

Full decomposition

An important issue is whether to use the first representation I or the second representation II to recover the density
\rho
. Ideally, we would like to use the second representation, for two reasons: (1) the static terms
R^{II}
contain additional favorable factors at low frequencies, compared to the terms
R^{I}
, and (2) the derivative
\partial_{s}h
of the “profile"
h
is expected to be smaller than the profile itself.

However, the representation II contains the potentially small denominator
1-\langle v,\xi\rangle-i|\xi|
, coming from the normal form. To avoid the associated singularity, our basic idea is to use the representation I when this denominator is small (essentially
\big|1-\langle v,\xi\rangle-i|\xi|\big|\lesssim 1
) and then use the representation II when the denominator is large. More precisely we define the sets

\begin{split}A^{I}&:=\big\{(j,k,m)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}:\,m<\delta^{-4}\text{ or }j>19m/20\text{ or }k+j+\delta m/3>0\big\},\\
A^{II}&:=\big\{(j,k,m)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}:\,m\geq\delta^{-4}\text{ and }j\leq 19m/20\text{ and }k+j+\delta m/3\leq 0\big\},\end{split}

(2.35)

where
\delta:=10^{-4}
is a small parameter. We have thus established the following full decomposition of the density
\rho
:

Proposition 2.3
.

Assume that
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a regular solution of the system (
2.1
) and define the function
\rho
as before. Then we can decompose

\rho=\rho^{stat}+\Re\big\{e^{-it}\rho^{osc}\big\},

(2.36)

where, with the definitions (
2.26
)–(
2.29
) and (
2.31
)–(
2.35
)

\begin{split}\rho^{stat}(x,t)=\sum_{\ast\in\{I,II\}}\sum_{(j,k,m)\in A^{\ast}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{m}(t)[R^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(x,t)+R^{\ast}_{2,j,k}(x,t)],\\
\rho^{osc}(x,t)=\sum_{\ast\in\{I,II\}}\sum_{(j,k,m)\in A^{\ast}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{m}(t)[T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(x,t)+T^{\ast}_{2,j,k}(x,t)].\end{split}

(2.37)

The main point of this decomposition is that we will be able to prove stronger control of the low frequencies of the stationary component
\rho^{stat}
compared to the time-oscillatory component
\rho^{osc}
. See Proposition
2.4
below.

2.3.
Norms and the bootstrap proposition

We are now ready to define our main norms and state the main bootstrap proposition. Let
B_{T}
denote the space of continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]
defined by the norm

\begin{split}&\|f\|_{B_{T}}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|f(t)\|_{B^{0}_{t}},\\
&\|f(t)\|_{B^{0}_{t}}:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\big\{\langle t\rangle^{3}\|P_{k}f(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|P_{k}f(t)\|_{L^{1}}\big\}.\end{split}

(2.38)

Assume that
\delta=10^{-4}
is a small parameter and define the norms

\begin{split}\|f\|_{Stat_{\delta}}&:=\|\langle t\rangle^{1-2\delta}\langle\nabla_{x}\rangle f(t)\|_{B_{T}},\\
\|f\|_{Osc_{\delta}}&:=\|\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}f(t)\|_{B_{T}}+\|\langle t\rangle^{1-2\delta}\nabla_{x,t}f(t)\|_{B_{T}}.\end{split}

(2.39)

We are now ready to state our main bootstrap proposition:

Proposition 2.4
.

There exist
\overline{\varepsilon}\in(0,1]
and
C\geq 1
such that the following is true:

Assume that
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a regular solution of the system (
2.1
) for some
T>0
with initial data
f(0)=f_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying the smallness condition

\|\langle v\rangle^{4.5}(\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\partial_{v}^{\beta}f_{0})(x,v)\|_{(L^{\infty}_{x}\cap L^{1}_{x})L^{\infty}_{v}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}\leq\overline{\varepsilon},\quad\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{3},\quad\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert\leq 1.

(2.40)

Assume that the associated density
\rho
decomposes as
\rho=\rho^{stat}+\Re\{e^{-it}\rho^{osc}\}
as in Proposition
2.3
, where for some
0<\varepsilon_{1}\leq\varepsilon_{0}^{3/4}

\|\rho^{stat}\|_{Stat_{\delta}}+\|\rho^{osc}\|_{Osc_{\delta}}\leq\varepsilon_{1}.

(2.41)

Then the functions
\rho^{stat}
and
\rho^{osc}
satisfy the improved bounds

\|\rho^{stat}\|_{Stat_{\delta}}+\|\rho^{osc}\|_{Osc_{\delta}}\leq\varepsilon_{0}+C\varepsilon_{1}^{3/2}.

(2.42)

The proof of Proposition
2.4
will take up sections 4, 5, 6, and 7. We decompose

\begin{split}\rho^{stat}&=\rho^{stat}_{1,I}+\rho^{stat}_{1,II}+\rho^{stat}_{2,I}+\rho^{stat}_{2,II},\\
\rho^{osc}&=\rho^{osc}_{1,I}+\rho^{osc}_{1,II}+\rho^{osc}_{2,I}+\rho^{osc}_{2,II},\\
\end{split}

(2.43)

where for
a\in\{1,2\}
and
\ast\in\{I,II\}
we define

\begin{split}\rho^{stat}_{a,\ast}(x,t)&:=\sum_{(j,k,m)\in A^{\ast}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{m}(t)R^{\ast}_{a,j,k}(x,t),\\
\rho^{osc}_{a,\ast}(x,t)&:=\sum_{(j,k,m)\in A^{\ast}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{m}(t)T^{\ast}_{a,j,k}(x,t).\end{split}

(2.44)

Finally, in Section
8
we show how Proposition
2.4
implies our main result Theorem
1.1
.

3.
Preliminary estimates

In this section we assume that
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a regular solution of the system (
2.1
), and the density function
\rho
satisfies the bootstrap assumptions (
2.41
).

To measure the deviation of the characteristic flow from the free flow we define the functions
\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{W}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{I}^{2}_{T}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
by

\begin{split}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)&:=X(x+tv,v,s,t)-x-sv,\\
\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)&:=V(x+tv,v,s,t)-v.\end{split}

(3.1)

The definitions (
2.3
) show that
\widetilde{Y}(x,v,t,t)=0,\widetilde{W}(x,v,t,t)=0
and

\begin{split}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)&=-\int_{s}^{t}E(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)&=\int_{s}^{t}(\tau-s)E(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(3.2)

Moreover, for any
s\leq t\in[0,T]
and
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\partial_{s}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)=\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t),\qquad\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)=-\int_{s}^{t}\widetilde{W}(x,v,\tau,t)\,d\tau.

(3.3)

3.1.
Bounds on the density function
\rho
and electric field E

The decomposition
\rho=\rho^{stat}+\Re\{e^{-it}\rho^{osc}\}
in Proposition
2.3
induces a natural decomposition of the electric field

\begin{split}&E(t)=E^{stat}(t)+\Re\{e^{-it}E^{osc}(t)\},\\
&E^{stat}(t):=(\nabla_{x}\Delta_{x}^{-1}\rho^{stat})(t),\qquad E^{osc}(t):=(\nabla_{x}\Delta_{x}^{-1}\rho^{osc})(t).\end{split}

(3.4)

We start with some bounds on the density components
\rho^{stat}
,
\rho^{osc}
and on the electric fields
E^{stat}
,
E^{osc}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

(i) For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t\in[0,T]
we have

\langle t\rangle^{3}\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-k^{+}}\langle t\rangle^{-1+2\delta},

(3.5)

\langle t\rangle^{3}\|(P_{k}\rho^{osc})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(P_{k}\rho^{osc})(t)\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{\langle t\rangle^{1-2\delta}2^{k}+\langle t\rangle^{-\delta}},

(3.6)

\langle t\rangle^{3}\|(P_{k}\nabla_{x,t}\rho^{osc})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(P_{k}\nabla_{x,t}\rho^{osc})(t)\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-1+2\delta}.

(3.7)

(ii) Let
R_{j}
denote the Riesz transforms
R_{j}=\partial_{j}|\nabla|^{-1}
,
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then, for any
t\in[0,T]

\begin{split}&\|E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\|R_{j}E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-3+2\delta},\\
&\|\nabla_{x}E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-4+2\delta}\ln(2+t),\end{split}

(3.8)

and

\begin{split}&\|E^{osc}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\|R_{j}E^{osc}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta},\\
&\|\nabla_{x}E^{osc}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-3+\delta}\ln(2+t),\qquad\|\partial_{t}E^{osc}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-3+2\delta}.\end{split}

(3.9)

Proof.

The bounds (
3.5
)–(
3.7
) follow directly from the bootstrap assumptions (
2.41
) and the definitions (
2.38
)–(
2.39
). To prove (
3.8
)–(
3.9
) we use (
3.5
)–(
3.7
) and the simple estimates
\|P_{k}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{3k}\|P_{k}g\|_{L^{1}}
. Therefore

\begin{split}&\|E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\|R_{j}E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\lesssim\sum_{2^{k}\geq\langle t\rangle^{-1}}2^{-k}\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{2^{k}\leq\langle t\rangle^{-1}}2^{2k}\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-3+2\delta}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}\|\nabla_{x}E^{stat}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}&\lesssim\sum_{2^{k}\geq\langle t\rangle^{-1}}\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{2^{k}\leq\langle t\rangle^{-1}}2^{3k}\|(P_{k}\rho^{stat})(t)\|_{L^{1}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-4+2\delta}\ln(2+t).\end{split}

The bounds (
3.8
) follow. The bounds (
3.9
) are similar, using (
3.6
)–(
3.7
).
∎

3.2.
Fourier multipliers

Some of our main identities involve Fourier multipliers, such as the “derivative” operators
\mathcal{D}
. To estimate these contributions efficiently we need to localize in the Fourier space.

For any
d\geq 1
let
S^{\infty}=S^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of continuous compactly supported multipliers
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by the norm

\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}:=\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}m\|_{L^{1}}<\infty.

(3.10)

Notice that if
m,m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then

\|mm^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}\leq\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}.

(3.11)

Moreover if
m\in S^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, then

\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(m\widehat{f})\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{p}}.

(3.12)

We often use integration by parts to estimate oscillatory integrals with non-degenerate phases, according to the following general lemma:

Lemma 3.2
.

Assume that
0<\varepsilon\leq 1/\varepsilon\leq K
,
N\geq 1
is an integer, and
f,g\in C^{N}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
d\geq 1
. Then

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{iKf}g\,dx\Big|\lesssim_{N}(K\varepsilon)^{-N}\big[\sum_{|\alpha|\leq N}\varepsilon^{|\alpha|}\|D^{\alpha}_{x}g\|_{L^{1}}\big],

(3.13)

provided that
f
is real-valued,

|\nabla_{x}f|\geq\mathbf{1}_{{\mathrm{supp}}\,g},\quad\text{ and }\quad\|D_{x}^{\alpha}f\cdot\mathbf{1}_{{\mathrm{supp}}\,g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{N}\varepsilon^{1-|\alpha|},\,2\leq|\alpha|\leq N+1.

(3.14)

We define the resonant projectors
\Pi_{\iota,k,p}
,
\iota\in\{+1,-1\}
,
k\leq 4
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, by the Fourier multipliers
\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{p}(1+\iota v\cdot\xi)
, i.e.

\Pi_{\iota,k,p}f(x,v):=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\big\{\widehat{f}(\xi)\cdot\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{p}(1+\iota v\cdot\xi)\big\}(x,v).

(3.15)

Similarly, we define the projectors
\Pi_{\iota,k,\leq p}
,
\Pi_{\iota,k,>p}
,
k\leq 4
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. These projectors are important in arguments involving normal forms. We define also the functions

\omega_{\iota,\kappa}(\xi,v):=1+\iota v\cdot\xi+i\kappa|\xi|,\qquad\iota\in\{-1,1\},\,\kappa\in\{-1,0,1\},

(3.16)

and the operators
\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}
defined by the multipliers
(\xi,v)\to\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}(\xi,v)
. We prove the following:

Lemma 3.3
.

(i) If
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,
k\leq 4
and
p\leq-4
then

\Pi_{\iota,k,\leq p}g(x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(x-y)K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(y,v)\,dy,

(3.17)

for some kernels
K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}
that satisfy the bounds

\begin{split}&\sup_{|v|\in[2^{l-1},2^{l+1}]}\|K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(.,v)\|_{L^{1}_{y}}\lesssim 1,\qquad\sup_{|v|\in[2^{l-1},2^{l+1}]}\|K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(.,v)\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}}\lesssim 2^{2k}2^{p-l},\end{split}

(3.18)

for any
l\in\mathbb{Z}
. Moreover,
\mathfrak{1}_{[2^{l-1},2^{l+1}]}(|v|)K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(y,v)\equiv 0
if
k+l\leq-4
.

As a consequence, for any
q\leq r\in[1,\infty]
and
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

\sup_{|v|\in[2^{l-1},2^{l+1}]}\|\Pi_{\iota,k,\leq p}g(.,v)\|_{L^{r}_{x}}\lesssim 2^{(2k+p-l)(1/q-1/r)}\|g\|_{L^{q}_{x}}.

(3.19)

(ii) If
\iota\in\{-1,1\}
,
\kappa\in\{-1,0,1\}
,
k\leq 4
, and
p\in\mathbb{Z}
then

(\Pi_{\iota,k,p}\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}g)(x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}g(x-y)\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(y,v)\,dy,

(3.20)

for some kernels
\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}
that satisfy the bounds

\|\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(.,v)\|_{L^{1}_{y}}\lesssim 2^{-p},

(3.21)

for any
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. As a consequence, for any
q\in[1,\infty]
,
g\in L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\|\Pi_{\iota,k,p}\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}g(.,v)\|_{L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim 2^{-p}\|g\|_{L^{q}_{x}}.

(3.22)

Proof.

(i) We have the formula

K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(y,v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{iy\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{\leq p}(1+\iota v\cdot\xi)\,d\xi.

(3.23)

Clearly
K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(y,v)=0
if
2^{k}|v|\leq 1/8
and
p\leq-4
. On the other hand, if
2^{k}|v|\geq 1/8
and
p\leq-4
then we may assume
v=(v_{1},0,0)
,
v_{1}\in[2^{l-1},2^{l+1}]
, and integrate by parts (using Lemma
3.2
) to see that

\big|K_{k,\leq p}^{\iota}(y,v)\big|\lesssim 2^{2k+p-l}(1+2^{k}|y_{2}|+2^{k}|y_{3}|+2^{p-l}|y_{1}|)^{-4}.

The
L^{1}
bounds in (
3.18
) follow as well, and the bounds (
3.19
) follow by interpolation and Hólder’s inequality.

(ii) We may assume that
v=(v_{1},0,0)
,
v_{1}\geq 0
. Notice that

\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(y,v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{iy\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\frac{\varphi_{0}(2^{-p}(1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}))}{1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}+i\kappa|\xi|}\,d\xi.

(3.24)

Notice that, for any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in[0,4]
and
\xi
in the support of the integral in (
3.24
),

\Big|\frac{d^{\alpha_{1}}}{d\xi_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}}\frac{d^{\alpha_{2}}}{d\xi_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}}\frac{d^{\alpha_{3}}}{d\xi_{3}^{\alpha_{3}}}\Big(\varphi_{k}(\xi)\frac{\varphi_{0}(2^{-p}(1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}))}{1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}+i\kappa|\xi|}\Big)\Big|\lesssim(2^{p}+|\kappa|2^{k})^{-1}2^{-k(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})}(2^{-k}+|v_{1}|2^{-p})^{\alpha_{1}}.

(3.25)

Therefore, using integration by parts (Lemma
3.2
), for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\big|\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(y,v)\big|\lesssim 2^{-p}2^{2k}(2^{-k}+2^{-p}|v_{1}|)^{-1}\big\{1+2^{k}|y_{2}|+2^{k}|y_{3}|+(2^{-k}+2^{-p}|v_{1}|)^{-1}|y_{1}|\big\}^{-4}.

(3.26)

In particular
\|\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(.,v)\|_{L^{1}_{y}}\lesssim 2^{-p}
, and the desired bounds (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) follow.
∎

3.3.
Linear dispersive estimates

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and multipliers
\mathfrak{m}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}
we define the operators

\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)(x):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-i\lambda v\cdot\xi}\widehat{g}(\xi,v)\mathfrak{m}(\xi,v)\,d\xi dv.

(3.27)

We have the following lemma:

Lemma 3.4
.

(i) If
j\in\{1,2,3\}
and
\lambda\neq 0
then

\mathcal{I}_{i\xi_{j}\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)=\frac{1}{\lambda}\big\{\mathcal{I}_{\partial_{v_{j}}\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)+\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\partial_{v_{j}}g;\lambda)\big\}.

(3.28)

(ii) Let
K
denote the inverse Fourier transform in
\xi
of the multiplier
\mathfrak{m}
,

K(y,v):=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathfrak{m}(\xi,v)e^{iy\cdot\xi}\,d\xi.

(3.29)

Then, if
q\in[1,\infty]
,

\begin{split}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)\|_{L^{q}_{x}}&\lesssim\|g\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{q}_{y}}\|K\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{y}},\\
|\lambda|^{3}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\|g\|_{X_{\lambda}}\|K\|_{L^{1}_{y}L^{\infty}_{v}},\end{split}

(3.30)

where, by definition,

\|g\|_{X_{\lambda}}:=\sup_{p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|g(w,p-w/\lambda)|\,dw.

(3.31)

(iii) For
k\in\mathbb{Z}
let
\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\xi,v):=\mathfrak{m}(\xi,v)\varphi_{k}(\xi)
and
K_{k}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathfrak{m}_{k})
. Then, if
q\in[1,\infty]
,

\begin{split}|\lambda|\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}_{k}}(g;\lambda)\|_{L^{q}_{x}}&\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\{\|g\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{q}_{y}}\|\nabla_{v}K_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{y}}+\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{q}_{y}}\|K_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{y}}\big\},\\
|\lambda|^{4}\|\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}_{k}}(g;\lambda)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim 2^{-k}\big\{\|g\|_{X_{\lambda}}\|\nabla_{v}K_{k}\|_{L^{1}_{y}L^{\infty}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{v}g\|_{X_{\lambda}}\|K_{k}\|_{L^{1}_{y}L^{\infty}_{v}}\big\}.\end{split}

(3.32)

Proof.

(i) The identities (
3.28
) follow from the definition and integration by parts in
v
.

(ii) To prove the bounds (
3.30
) we rewrite

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{\mathfrak{m}}(g;\lambda)(x)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(y,v)g(x-y-\lambda v,v)\,dydv\\
&=|\lambda|^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}K\big(y,\frac{x-y-w}{\lambda}\big)g\big(w,\frac{x-y-w}{\lambda}\big)\,dydw.\end{split}

(3.33)

The bounds in (
3.30
) follow from these identities.

(iii) To prove (
3.32
) we write

\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\xi,v)=\xi_{a}\cdot\mathfrak{m}_{k}(\xi,v)\cdot(\xi_{a}/|\xi|^{2})\varphi_{[k-2,k+2]}(\xi),

and then combine the identity (
3.28
) and the first two bounds in (
3.30
).
∎

We will also need dispersive bounds on the resonant projectors
\Pi_{\iota,k,\leq p}
and
\Pi_{\iota,k,p}\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\iota\in\{1,-1\}
,
\kappa\in\{-1,0,1\}
,
k\leq 4
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|\lambda|\geq 1+2^{-p(1+\delta)}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
then

\begin{split}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|(\Pi_{\iota,k,\leq p}g)(x+\lambda v,v)\big|\,dv&\lesssim|\lambda|^{-3}\|g\|_{L^{1}},\\
\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|(\Pi_{\iota,k,p}\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}g)(x+\lambda v,v)\big|\,dv&\lesssim 2^{-p}|\lambda|^{-3}\|g\|_{L^{1}}.\end{split}

(3.34)

Proof.

The two estimates are similar, so we focus on the harder bounds in the second line. Using (
3.20
) we have

\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|(\Pi_{\iota,k,p}\omega_{\iota,\kappa}^{-1}g)(x+\lambda v,v)\big|\,dv\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}|g(y)\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(x+\lambda v-y,v)|\,dvdy.

To prove the bounds in the second line of (
3.34
) it suffices to show that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(x+\lambda v,v)\big|\,dv\lesssim 2^{-p}|\lambda|^{-3}.

(3.35)

As in (
3.24
) we write

\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(x+\lambda v,v)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{i(x+\lambda v)\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\frac{\varphi_{0}(2^{-p}(1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}))}{1+\iota v_{1}\xi_{1}+i\kappa|\xi|}\,d\xi.

(3.36)

Let
H_{p}^{\iota,\kappa}
denote the Fourier transform of part of the integrand,

H_{p}^{\kappa}(\xi,\rho):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\rho\beta}\frac{\varphi_{0}(\beta)}{2^{p}\beta+i\kappa|\xi|}\,d\beta,

(3.37)

therefore, for any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,

\frac{\varphi_{0}(\beta)}{2^{p}\beta+i\kappa|\xi|}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\rho\beta}H_{p}^{\kappa}(\xi,\rho)\,d\rho.

(3.38)

We substitute this identity into (
3.36
), with
\beta=2^{-p}(1+\iota v\cdot\xi)
, so

\begin{split}\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(x+\lambda v,v)&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i(x+\lambda v)\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)e^{i\rho 2^{-p}(1+\iota v\cdot\xi)}H_{p}^{\kappa}(\xi,\rho)\,d\rho d\xi\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\rho 2^{-p}}G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(x+\lambda v+\iota\rho 2^{-p}v,\rho)\,d\rho,\end{split}

(3.39)

where, using also the definition (
3.37
),

\begin{split}G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(y,\rho)&:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{iy\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)H_{p}^{\kappa}(\xi,\rho)\,d\xi\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{iy\cdot\xi}e^{-i\rho\beta}\frac{\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{0}(\beta)}{2^{p}\beta+i\kappa|\xi|}\,d\beta d\xi.\end{split}

(3.40)

We notice that

\Big|D^{a}_{\beta}D^{\alpha}_{\xi}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{k}(\xi)\varphi_{0}(\beta)}{2^{p}\beta+i\kappa|\xi|}\Big)\Big|\lesssim_{N}2^{-p}2^{-|\alpha|k}

(3.41)

if
|\xi|\in[2^{k-1},2^{k+1}]
,
|\beta|\in[1/2,2]
,
a\leq N
, and
|\alpha|\leq 4
. We can therefore integrate by parts in the formula (
3.40
) to see that

|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(y,\rho)|\lesssim_{N}2^{-p}2^{3k}(1+2^{k}|y|)^{-4}(1+|\rho|)^{-N}\qquad\text{ for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\,\rho\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.42)

We can now prove the bounds (
3.35
). For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we use the formula (
3.39
) to estimate

\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|\widetilde{K}_{k,p}^{\iota,\kappa}(x+\lambda v,v)\big|\,dv\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(x+\lambda v+\iota\rho 2^{-p}v,\rho)\big|\,dvd\rho\lesssim I+II,

where

\begin{split}I&:=\int_{|\rho|\leq 2^{p}|\lambda|/2}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(x+(\lambda+\iota\rho 2^{-p})v,\rho)\big|\,dvd\rho,\\
II&:=\int_{|\rho|\geq 2^{p}|\lambda|/2}\int_{|v|\leq|\lambda|^{8}}\big|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(x+(\lambda+\iota\rho 2^{-p})v,\rho)\big|\,dvd\rho.\end{split}

It follows from (
3.42
) that
\|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(.,\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{y}}\lesssim 2^{-p}(1+|\rho|)^{-2}
. Moreover
|\lambda+\iota\rho 2^{-p}|\in[|\lambda|/2,2|\lambda|]
if
|\rho|\leq 2^{p}|\lambda|/2
. We can therefore make a suitable change of variables and integrate in
v
to see that
|I|\lesssim 2^{-p}|\lambda|^{-3}
. In addition, it follows from (
3.42
) that
\|G_{k,p}^{\kappa}(.,\rho)\|_{L^{\infty}_{y}}\lesssim_{N}2^{-p}(1+|\rho|)^{-N}
. Notice that
2^{p}|\lambda|\gtrsim|\lambda|^{\delta/2}
, due to the assumption
|\lambda|\geq 1+2^{-p(1+\delta)}
. We can thus take
N
large enough to see that
|II|\lesssim 2^{-p}|\lambda|^{-3}
and the desired bounds (
3.35
) follow.
∎

3.3.1.
The
S^{\infty}L^{\infty}
class

To apply dispersive bounds in certain cases, we need to introduce the space
S^{\infty}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
of multipliers
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by the norm

\|m\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}:=\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}m\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{v}}=\Big\|\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}m(\xi,v)e^{ix\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\Big|\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}}.

(3.43)

Lemma 3.6
.

(i) If
m,m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}L^{\infty}
then
mm^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}L^{\infty}
and

\|mm^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\leq\|m\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\|m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}.

(3.44)

(ii) If
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
then

\|\mathcal{D}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{j+k},

(3.45)

\|(\nabla_{v}\mathcal{D})\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k}.

(3.46)

Moreover, if
j+k\leq-10
then

\big\|(1+\mathcal{D}^{2})^{-1}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\big\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1.

(3.47)

Proof.

The bounds (
3.44
), (
3.45
), and (
3.46
) follow directly from definitions. To prove (
3.47
) we decompose
(1+\mathcal{D}^{2})=(\mathcal{D}+i)(\mathcal{D}-i)
and integrate by parts in
\xi
to show that

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}(\xi,v)\pm i}e^{ix\cdot\xi}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\,d\xi\Big|\lesssim\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)2^{3k}(1+2^{k}|x|)^{-4}

for any
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The bounds (
3.47
) follow.
∎

3.4.
Pointwise estimates of characteristics

We prove now estimates on the functions
\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{W}
defined in (
3.1
), which measure the nonlinear deviation of the characteristic flow.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
(s,t)\in\mathcal{I}_{T}^{2}=\{(s,t)\in[0,T]^{2}:\,0\leq s\leq t\leq T\}
we have

\sup_{x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)-(t-s)E(x+tv,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t-s\rangle\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}\langle s\rangle^{-1+1.1\delta},

(3.48)

\sup_{x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\partial_{t}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)+E(x+tv,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t-s\rangle\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}\langle s\rangle^{-2+1.1\delta}.

(3.49)

Proof.

It follows from (
3.2
) and the assumption
\widetilde{Y}(x,v,t,t)=0
that

\begin{split}\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)&=(t-s)E(x+tv,t)\\
&+\int_{s}^{t}(\tau-s)\nabla_{x}E(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,v),\tau)\cdot\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t)\,d\tau\end{split}

(3.50)

and

\partial_{t}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)=-E(x+tv,t)-\int_{s}^{t}\nabla_{x}E(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,v),\tau)\cdot\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t)\,d\tau.

(3.51)

For
t\in[0,T]
let

Z_{0}(t):=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sup_{x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}({t-s})^{-1}|\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|.

Using (
3.50
) and Lemma
3.1
(ii) we have

\begin{split}Z_{0}(t)&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}+\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{t-s}\int_{s}^{t}(\tau-s)\langle\tau\rangle^{-3+2\delta}(t-\tau)Z_{0}(t)d\tau\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}+\varepsilon_{1}\int_{s}^{t}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2+2\delta}Z_{0}(t)d\tau\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{0}(t).\end{split}

Therefore
Z_{0}(t)\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}
. Using again (
3.50
) and Lemma
3.1
(ii) we have

\begin{split}|\partial_{t}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)-(t-s)E(x+tv,t)|&\lesssim\int_{s}^{t}\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-3+1.1\delta}\langle\tau-s\rangle\langle t-\tau\rangle\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}\,d\tau\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle t-s\rangle\langle t\rangle^{-2+\delta}\langle s\rangle^{-1+1.1\delta},\end{split}

which gives the desired estimate (
3.48
). The estimates (
3.49
) follow in a similar way.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
(s,t)\in\mathcal{I}^{2}_{T}
and
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
we have

\begin{split}|\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-1+2\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-1/6}\},\\
|\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-2+2\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-7/6}\},\end{split}

(3.52)

\begin{split}|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-2+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-7/6}\},\\
|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-3+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-13/6}\},\end{split}

(3.53)

\begin{split}|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-1+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-1/6}\},\\
|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-2+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-7/6}\}.\end{split}

(3.54)

Proof.

Step 1.
To prove the bounds (
3.52
) we use the decomposition (
3.4
) of the electric field, and rewrite the identity in the first line of (
3.2
) in the form

\begin{split}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)&=\widetilde{W}^{stat}(x,v,s,t;t)+\widetilde{W}^{osc}(x,v,s,t;t),\\
\widetilde{W}^{stat}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\big[E^{stat}+\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{\geq 0}E^{osc})\big](x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{W}^{osc}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{<0}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(3.55)

In view of (
3.6
) we have
\|P_{\geq 0}E^{osc}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-4+2\delta}
. Thus, using also (
3.8
), if
m\geq 0
and
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
then

|\widetilde{W}^{stat}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-3+2\delta}\,d\tau\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-2m+2\delta m}.

(3.56)

To bound
|\widetilde{W}^{osc}|
we integrate by parts in
\tau
. Notice that
\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t)=\widetilde{W}(x,v,\tau,t)
, see (
3.3
), and
|\widetilde{W}(x,v,\tau,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-1+\delta}
(due to (
3.2
) and (
3.8
)–(
3.9
)). Therefore

\begin{split}|\widetilde{W}^{osc}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|&\lesssim\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\big\{\|(\partial_{\tau}P_{<0}E^{osc})(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|(\nabla_{x}P_{<0}E^{osc})(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\qquad\quad\,\,\times(|v|+|\widetilde{W}(x,v,\tau,t)|)\big\}\,d\tau+\sum_{\tau\in\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}\|(P_{<0}E^{osc})(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-2m+2\delta m}\langle v\rangle,\end{split}

(3.57)

using (
3.9
) again in the last line.

We prove now that if
m\geq 0
,
|v|\geq 2^{4m/5+10}
, and
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
then

|\widetilde{W}^{osc}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-7m/6}.

(3.58)

Indeed, we decompose

\begin{split}&\widetilde{W}^{osc}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)=-\sum_{k\leq-1}\Re I^{(1)}_{k}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t),\\
&I^{(1)}_{k}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t):=\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}e^{-i\tau}(P_{k}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(3.59)

Using (
3.6
) we have
\|P_{k}E^{osc}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle\tau\rangle^{-3},2^{3k}\}\langle\tau\rangle^{-1+2\delta}2^{-2k}
for any
k\leq 0
, thus

\sum_{k\leq-6m/5+60}|I^{(1)}_{k}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\sum_{k\leq-6m/5+60}\varepsilon_{1}2^{2\delta m}2^{k}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-6m/5+2\delta m},

(3.60)

for any
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
, consistent with the desired bounds in (
3.58
).

On the other hand, if
k\in[-6m/5+60,0]
(in particular
m\geq 50
) then we write

e^{-i\tau}(P_{k}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{-i\tau}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widehat{E^{osc}}(\xi,\tau)e^{i\xi\cdot(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t))}\,d\xi.

We insert cutoff functions of the form
\varphi_{\leq p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)
and
\varphi_{\leq p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)
, and decompose

\begin{split}I^{(1)}_{k}&=J^{(1)}_{k,\leq p_{0}}+J^{(1)}_{k,>p_{0}},\\
J^{(1)}_{k,\ast}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\varphi_{\ast}(1-\xi\cdot v)e^{-i\tau(1-\xi\cdot v)}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widehat{E^{osc}}(\xi,\tau)e^{i\xi\cdot(x+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t))}\,d\xi d\tau,\end{split}

(3.61)

where
p_{0}:=-2m/5
and
\ast\in\{\leq p_{0},>p_{0}\}
. We have
\|\widehat{P_{k}E^{osc}}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|P_{k}E^{osc}(\tau)\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-2k}\langle\tau\rangle^{-1+2\delta}
, using (
3.6
). Recalling that
|v|\geq 2^{4m/5+10}
, we have

\big|J^{(1)}_{k,\leq p_{0}}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)\big|\lesssim\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\varphi_{\leq p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)||\varphi_{k}(\xi)||\widehat{E^{osc}}(\xi,\tau)|\,d\xi d\tau\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-6m/5+2\delta m}.

(3.62)

On the other hand, to bound
|J^{(1)}_{k,>p_{0}}|
we integrate by parts in
\tau
and estimate

\begin{split}|J^{(1)}_{k,>p_{0}}&(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\negmedspace\sum_{\tau\in\{s_{1},s_{2}\}}\negmedspace\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\varphi_{>p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)}{1-\xi\cdot v}e^{-i\tau(1-\xi\cdot v)}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widehat{E^{osc}}(\xi,\tau)e^{i\xi\cdot(x+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t))}\,d\xi\Big|\\
&+\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\varphi_{>p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)}{1-\xi\cdot v}e^{-i\tau(1-\xi\cdot v)}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\frac{d}{d\tau}\big\{\widehat{E^{osc}}(\xi,\tau)e^{i\xi\cdot(x+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t))}\big\}\,d\xi\Big|d\tau.\end{split}

In view of (
3.22
), for
F\in\{E^{osc}(\tau),\,(\partial_{\tau}E^{osc})(\tau)\}
we have

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\varphi_{>p_{0}}(1-\xi\cdot v)}{1-\xi\cdot v}e^{-i\tau(1-\xi\cdot v)}\varphi_{k}(\xi)\widehat{F}(\xi)e^{i\xi\cdot(x+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t))}\,d\xi\Big|\lesssim 2^{-p_{0}}\|P_{k}F\|_{L^{\infty}}.

Since
\|P_{k}E^{osc}(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-2+2\delta}
and
\|P_{k}(\partial_{\tau}E^{osc})(\tau)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-3+2\delta}
(due to (
3.6
)–(
3.7
)) and
|\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t)|=|\widetilde{W}(x,v,\tau,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\langle\tau\rangle^{-1+\delta}
, it follows from the last two estimates that

|J^{(1)}_{k,>p_{0}}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-p_{0}}2^{-2m+4\delta m}

(3.63)

for any
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
.

Recalling that
p_{0}=-2m/5
and using (
3.61
)–(
3.62
) it follows that
|I^{(1)}_{k}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-6m/5+2\delta m}
. The desired bounds (
3.58
) follow using also (
3.60
).

We combine now the bounds (
3.56
)–(
3.58
) and divide the interval
[s,t]
dyadically to conclude that
|\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-2+2\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-7/6}\}
for any
s\leq t\in[0,T]
. This gives the bounds in the second line of (
3.52
). The bounds in the first line then follow using the identity
\partial_{s}\widetilde{Y}=\widetilde{W}
and integrating on the interval
[s,t]
.

Step 2.
To prove the bounds (
3.53
) we define, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

Z_{1}(t):=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sup_{x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big[\langle v\rangle^{-1}\langle s\rangle^{2-2.1\delta}+\langle s\rangle^{7/6}\big]\big[|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|+\langle s\rangle\nabla_{x}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|\big].

(3.64)

In view of (
3.2
) we have

\partial_{x^{a}}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)=-\int_{s}^{t}(\partial_{x^{b}}E)(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)(\delta_{ab}+\partial_{x^{a}}\widetilde{Y}_{b}(x,v,\tau,t))\,d\tau,

so we can decompose, as in (
3.55
)

\begin{split}\partial_{x^{a}}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)&=\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,1}(x,v,s,t;t)+\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,2}(x,v,s,t;t)+\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{x}(x,v,s,t;t),\\
\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,1}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\big[\partial_{x^{b}}E^{stat}+\Re(e^{-i\tau}\partial_{x^{b}}P_{\geq 0}E^{osc})\big](x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(\delta_{ab}+\partial_{x^{a}}\widetilde{Y}_{b}(x,v,\tau,t))\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,2}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{<0}\partial_{x^{b}}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\partial_{x^{a}}\widetilde{Y}_{b}(x,v,\tau,t)\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{x}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{<0}\partial_{x^{a}}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau.\end{split}

(3.65)

Assume that
m\geq 0
and
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
. Since
|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t)|\leq Z_{1}(t)\langle\tau\rangle^{-7/6}
it follows from (
3.5
)–(
3.6
) that

|\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,1}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|+|\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{x,2}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-3m+2.1\delta m}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{1}(t)2^{-3m}.

(3.66)

Moreover, as in (
3.57
), we integrate by parts in
\tau
to show that

|\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{x}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2},t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-3m+2.1\delta m}\langle v\rangle.

(3.67)

Finally, one can estimate as in the proof of (
3.58
) to show that if
|v|\geq 2^{4m/5+10}
then

|\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{x}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-13m/6}.

(3.68)

We combine (
3.66
)–(
3.68
) and sum over integers
m
satisfying
2^{m}\gtrsim\langle s\rangle
to conclude that

|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-3+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-13/6}\}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{1}(t)\langle s\rangle^{-3}.

Using (
3.3
) it follows that

\langle s\rangle|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|+|\nabla_{x}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-2+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-7/6}\}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{1}(t)\langle s\rangle^{-2}.

(3.69)

In particular, using the definition (
3.64
),
Z_{1}(t)\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}
, and the desired bounds (
3.53
) follow.

Step 3.
The proof of the bounds (
3.54
) is similar. For
t\in[0,T]
we define

Z_{2}(t):=\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\sup_{x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\big[\langle v\rangle^{-1}\langle s\rangle^{1-2.1\delta}+\langle s\rangle^{1/6}\big]\big[|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|+\langle s\rangle\nabla_{v}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|\big].

(3.70)

Using the formulas (
3.2
) and the decomposition (
3.4
) we write

\partial_{v^{a}}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)=\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,1}(x,v,s,t;t)+\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,2}(x,v,s,t;t)+\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{v}(x,v,s,t;t),

(3.71)

where

\begin{split}\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,1}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\big[\partial_{x^{b}}E^{stat}+\Re(e^{-i\tau}\partial_{x^{b}}P_{\geq 0}E^{osc})\big](x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times(\tau\delta_{ab}+\partial_{v^{a}}\widetilde{Y}_{b}(x,v,\tau,t))\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,2}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{<0}\partial_{x^{b}}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\partial_{v^{a}}\widetilde{Y}_{b}(x,v,\tau,t)\,d\tau,\\
\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{v}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)&:=-\int_{s_{1}}^{s_{2}}\tau\Re(e^{-i\tau}P_{<0}\partial_{x^{a}}E^{osc})(x+\tau v+\widetilde{Y}(x,v,\tau,t),\tau)\,d\tau.\end{split}

As in (
3.66
), it follows from (
3.5
)–(
3.6
) that

|\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,1}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|+|\widetilde{W}^{stat}_{v,2}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2};t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}2^{-2m+2.1\delta m}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{2}(t)2^{-2m},

provided that
m\geq 0
and
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\in[2^{m}-1,2^{m+1}]\cap[0,t]
. Moreover, as in (
3.67
)–(
3.68
), we can integrate by parts in
\tau
to show that

|\widetilde{W}^{osc}_{v}(x,v,s_{1},s_{2},t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{2^{-2m+2.1\delta m}\langle v\rangle,2^{-7m/6}\}.

As before we combine these bounds, sum over integers
m
satisfying
2^{m}\gtrsim\langle s\rangle
, and use (
3.3
) to conclude that

\langle s\rangle|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{W}(x,v,s,t)|+|\nabla_{v}\widetilde{Y}(x,v,s,t)|\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}\min\{\langle s\rangle^{-1+2.1\delta}\langle v\rangle,\langle s\rangle^{-1/6}\}+\varepsilon_{1}Z_{2}(t)\langle s\rangle^{-1}.

In particular
Z_{2}(t)\lesssim\varepsilon_{1}
, using the definition (
3.70
), and the desired bounds (
3.54
) follow.
∎

4.
The contributions of the initial data

In this section we bound the contributions of the initial data
f_{0}
. These contributions are the terms
R^{\ast}_{1,j,k}
and
T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}
,
\ast\in\{I,II\}
, in the decomposition (
2.36
)–(
2.37
), originating from the term
L_{1}
defined in (
2.23
). Our main result in this section is the following:

Proposition 4.1
.

With the notation in (
2.43
)–(
2.44
), we have

\|\rho^{stat}_{1,I}\|_{Stat_{\delta}}+\|\rho^{stat}_{1,II}\|_{Stat_{\delta}}+\|\rho^{osc}_{1,I}\|_{Osc_{\delta}}+\|\rho^{osc}_{1,II}\|_{Osc_{\delta}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}.

(4.1)

We would like to use the defining formulas (
2.26
) and (
2.28
)–(
2.29
) and Lemma
3.4
(with
d=3
). For this we need several bounds on the functions
L_{1,j,k}
.

Lemma 4.2
.

With
L_{1,j,k}=L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)
defined as in (
2.23
)–(
2.24
), we have

\begin{split}&\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\big\{\big\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}L_{1,j,k}(s)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{x}}+\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}\big\|(\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k})(s)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{x}}\big\}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4.5j},\\
&\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\big\{\big\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}L_{1,j,k}(s)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}\big\|(\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k})(s)\big\|_{L^{\infty}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}\big\}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4.5j},\\
&\sup_{s\in[1,T]}\big\{\big\|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}L_{1,j,k}(s)\big\|_{X_{s}}+\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}\big\|(\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k})(s)\big\|_{X_{s}}\big\}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4.5j},\end{split}

(4.2)

for any
(j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}\times\mathbb{Z}
and multi-indices
\alpha,\beta
with
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 1
.

Proof.

We use the initial-data assumptions (
2.40
). Let

F_{0}(x,v):=|f_{0}(x,v)|+|\nabla_{x}f_{0}(x,v)|+|\nabla_{v}f_{0}(x,v)|,\qquad F_{0}^{\ast}(x):=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|F_{0}(x,v)|\langle v\rangle^{4.5}.

(4.3)

Notice that, for any
(x,v,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times[0,T]
we have

L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)=\widetilde{\varphi}_{j}(v)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}L_{1}(x-y,v,s)K_{k}(y)\,dy,

where
K_{k}:=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{k})
. Thus

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}&L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)|+\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}|\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)|\\
&\lesssim\widetilde{\varphi}_{[j-2,j+2]}(v)\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in\{Id,\nabla_{x},\nabla_{v},\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}\partial_{s}\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\mathcal{T}L_{1}(x-y,v,s)||K_{k}(y)|\,dy.\end{split}

(4.4)

We examine the formula in the first line of (
2.23
) and use the bounds (
3.48
), (
3.49
), (
3.53
), and (
3.54
). It follows that

|\mathcal{T}L_{1}(z,v,s)|\lesssim F_{0}(z+\widetilde{Y}(z,v,0,s),v+\widetilde{W}(z,v,0,t))\lesssim F_{0}^{\ast}(z+\widetilde{Y}(z,v,0,s))\langle v\rangle^{-4.5},

for any
\mathcal{T}\in\{Id,\nabla_{x},\nabla_{v},\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}\partial_{s}\}
. Using also (
4.4
) we have

\begin{split}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{v}^{\beta}&L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)|+\langle s\rangle^{1-\delta}|\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k}(x,v,s)|\\
&\lesssim\widetilde{\varphi}_{[j-2,j+2]}(v)\langle v\rangle^{-4.5}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}F_{0}^{\ast}(x-y+\widetilde{Y}(x-y,v,0,s))|K_{k}(y)|\,dy.\end{split}

(4.5)

The assumptions (
2.40
) show that
\|F_{0}^{\ast}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|F_{0}^{\ast}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}
. The
L^{\infty}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}
bounds in the second line of (
4.2
) then follow. The
L^{\infty}_{v}L^{1}_{x}
bounds in the first line also follow, once we notice that the mapping
x\mapsto x-y+\widetilde{Y}(x-y,v,0,s)
is a global change of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with Jacobian
\approx 1
for any
y,v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, due to the bounds (
3.53
).

Finally, the bounds on the
X_{s}
norms in the third line of (
4.2
) also follow using the definition (
3.31
) and the observation that the mapping
x\mapsto x-y+\widetilde{Y}(x-y,p-x/s,0,s)
is a global change of coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with Jacobian
\approx 1
for any
y,p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
s\geq 1
, due to the bounds (
3.53
)–(
3.54
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.
In view of the definitions, it suffices to prove that for
\ast\in\{I,II\}
,
(j,k,m)\in A^{\ast}
,
t\in[2^{m-1},2^{m+1}]
(or
t\in[0,2]
if
m=0
) we have

\displaystyle 2^{m(1-2\delta)}2^{k^{+}}\big[\|R^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+2^{3m}\|R^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-\delta^{2}j},

(4.6)

\displaystyle(2^{-\delta m}+2^{m(1-2\delta)}2^{k})\big[\|T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+2^{3m}\|T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-\delta^{2}j},

(4.7)

\displaystyle 2^{m(1-2\delta)}\big[\|(\partial_{t}+|\nabla|)T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+2^{3m}\|(\partial_{t}+|\nabla|)T^{\ast}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-\delta^{2}j}.

(4.8)

We prove these bounds in several steps.

Proof of (
4.6
).
Assume first that
\ast=I
, so we start from the formula (
2.26
). We use also Lemma
3.4
with
d=3
and
m(\xi,v)=\varphi_{[k-4,k+4]}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{[j-4,j+4]}(v)
,
\|m\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1
. Thus

\begin{split}2^{k^{+}}\|R^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{x}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-j},\\
2^{k^{+}}\|R^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-j},\\
\end{split}

(4.9)

using the bounds (
3.30
) and Lemma
4.2
. These bounds suffice if
2^{m}\lesssim 1
.

On the other hand, if
m\geq\delta^{-6}
, after using (
3.30
), (
3.32
), and Lemma
4.2
, we have

\begin{split}(1+2^{k}2^{m})\|R^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4j/3},\\
(1+2^{k}2^{m})2^{3m}\|R^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{X_{t}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{X_{t}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4j/3},\\
\end{split}

(4.10)

The desired bounds (
4.6
) follow for
\ast=I
, since
2^{4j/3}(1+2^{k+m})\gtrsim 2^{\delta^{2}j}2^{k^{+}}2^{m(1-2\delta)}
for any
(j,k,m)\in A^{I}
with
m\geq\delta^{-6}
.

Assume now that
\ast=II
, so we start from the formula (
2.28
). We use also Lemma
3.4
with
d=3
and

m^{\prime}(\xi,v)=\varphi_{[k-4,k+4]}(\xi)\widetilde{\varphi}_{[j-4,j+4]}(v)\frac{\mathcal{D}(\xi,v)^{2}}{1+\mathcal{D}(\xi,v)^{2}},

which satisfies
\|m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla_{v}m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{2j+2k}
if
j+k\leq-20
due to Lemma
3.6
. Thus, if
(j,k,m)\in A^{II}
(in particular
j+k\leq 0
), we have

\begin{split}(1+2^{k}2^{m})\|R^{II}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}&\lesssim 2^{k+j}\big[\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\big]\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4j/3}2^{k+j},\\
(1+2^{k}2^{m})2^{3m}\|R^{II}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim 2^{k+j}\big[\|L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{X_{t}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{X_{t}}\big]\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4j/3}2^{k+j}.\end{split}

Since
2^{j/3}2^{-k}(1+2^{k+m})\gtrsim 2^{\delta^{2}j}2^{k^{+}}2^{m(1-2\delta)}
for any
(j,k,m)\in A^{II}
, the desired bounds (
4.6
) follow if
\ast=II
.

Proof of (
4.7
) when
\ast=I
.
Using (
3.30
) and Lemma
4.2
we have

\begin{split}2^{k^{+}}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}&e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{x}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-j}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}2^{k^{+}}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}&e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\|L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla_{x}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{v}L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-j}\end{split}

for any
s\in[0,t]
. The bounds (
4.7
) follow if
\ast=I
and
2^{m}\lesssim 1
, using the identities (
2.26
).

On the other hand, if
m\geq\delta^{-6}
,
t\in[2^{m-2},2^{m+2}]
and
s\geq t/2
then we use (
3.30
), (
3.32
), and Lemma
4.2
to estimate

\begin{split}(1+2^{k+m})\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}\big[\|L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\big]\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}2^{-4j/3}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}(1+2^{k+m})2^{3m}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}\big[\|L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{X_{s}}+\|\nabla_{v}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{X_{s}}\big]\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}2^{-4j/3}.\end{split}

Moreover, if
s\in[0,t/2]
then we use (
3.30
) and Lemma
4.2
to estimate

\begin{split}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}&\lesssim(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}\|L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k+m})^{-6}2^{-4j/3}.\end{split}

Since
\|P_{l}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\widehat{P_{l}g}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim 2^{3l}\|\widehat{P_{l}g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{3l}\|P_{l}g\|_{L^{1}}
for any
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, it follows that

\begin{split}2^{3m}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}}(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k+m})^{-3}2^{-4j/3}.\end{split}

Therefore, using the identities (
2.26
) and the last four inequalities, we have

\begin{split}(1+2^{k+m})\big[\|T^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}+2^{3m}\|T^{I}_{1,j,k}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\big]&\lesssim\int_{0}^{t}\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-2}2^{-4j/3}\,ds\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}2^{-4j/3}\min\{2^{m},2^{-k}\}.\end{split}

Moreover,
2^{-4j/3}\min\{2^{m},2^{-k}\}\lesssim 2^{-\delta^{2}j}
if
(j,k,m)\in A^{I}
and
m\geq\delta^{-6}
, and the desired bounds (
4.7
) follow if
\ast=I
.

Proof of (
4.7
) when
\ast=II
.
Assume that
(j,k,m)\in A^{II}
, so
m\geq\delta^{-4}
, and
t\in[2^{m-2},2^{m+2}]
. If
s\in[0,t]
we use (
3.30
), Lemma
3.6
, and Lemma
4.2
to estimate

\begin{split}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\frac{\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}(\partial_{s}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}})(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}\|\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{L^{1}_{x}L^{1}_{v}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}2^{-j}\langle s\rangle^{-1+\delta},\end{split}

(4.11)

where
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\{1,\mathcal{D}\}
. If
s\geq t/2
then we use (
3.30
), Lemma
3.6
, and Lemma
4.2
to estimate

\begin{split}2^{3m}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\frac{\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}(\partial_{s}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}})(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}\|\partial_{s}L_{1,j,k}(s)\|_{X_{s}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-6}2^{-j}2^{-m+\delta m}.\end{split}

As before, since
\|P_{l}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{3l}\|P_{l}g\|_{L^{1}}
for any
l\in\mathbb{Z}
we can use (
4.11
) to estimate

\begin{split}2^{3m}\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}}&\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}e^{ix\cdot\xi}e^{-(t-s)|\xi|}e^{-isv\cdot\xi}\frac{\mathcal{D^{\prime}}}{1+\mathcal{D}^{2}}(\partial_{s}\widehat{L_{1,j,k}})(\xi,v,s)\,d\xi dv\Big\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\\
&\lesssim\varepsilon_{0}(1+2^{k}|t-s|)^{-3}2^{-j}\langle s\rangle^{-1+\delta}\end{split}

for
s\in[0,t/2]
. We use these last three inequalities and the formulas (
2.29
), and integrate from
0
to
t
